#10603. I'm having a much better time in GTA 6 now I've disabled its pesky Criminal Profile pings

Do you think you'd enjoy invasive UI elements in real life? You crouch to fuss your dog, and a little ping activates in your retina, informing you that you've just earned plus-three serotonin. Your boss chews you out for coming into work 15 minutes late thanks to a traffic accident holding you up on your way to work, a notification pings: "-10 percent motivation (lasts 24 hours)". Maybe you eat a bang-up meal, flirt with a hot stranger, beat your personal best on a run - ding, ding, ding. Well done, you win at being a human.

I think it'd be tortuous. I think it would interact with my already-questionable perception of reality, and send my neurodivergent coping mechanisms into overdrive. I already see time 'in blocks', and am incapable of breaking tasks down to anything less than 15 minute chunks - I can't imagine how ill I'd get if I could begin ascribing status buffs and debuffs to those blocks, too. We're all different, of course - such is the beauty of life - but I think most people would be pushed to the point of meltdown if every action they took had a quantifiable mechanical system attached to it.

So why is it any different in GTA 6? The game, after all, is the most effective simulation we've ever seen about modern life. Aside from the rampant crime sprees you can engage with, there's so much mundanity in the game it's beyond impressive: I spent the opening 10-or-so hours in the game with a very strict morning routine. I'd get up, work out, shower, feed Dolores the snake, drink a protein shake, then head to one of the local businesses to get some breakfast. Every day! I'm a busy person; I'm playing GTA 6 for fun, yes, but also as part of my job - I need to be making progress. Yet, Rockstar's invisible hand is so competently realised, so artisanally implemented, that I bow to its insistence that I live life properly and engage with all the systems suggested to me like a good little drone.

So, after being low-key brainwashed by the initial tutorialisation, it stands to reason that I'm open to Rockstar's other suggestions. And - right on cue - you get a mission early on that introduces you to what is perhaps the most hotly debated aspect of the game so far: the Criminal Profile. If you're unaware, this new mechanic is a bit like the Honour System in Red Dead Redemption 2. It's broken down into five levels: professional, hardened, violent, psycho, and irredeemable. If you hit that final bracket, irredeemable, you can never go back: it's essentially GTA 6's criminal version of 'being cancelled', only here there are real, serious consequences that will actually ruin your life forever.

The game tracks how you behave when you're playing as both Jason and Lucia. They have separate criminal profiles. If, for example, you fudge a robbery on a cartel's pill-shipping operation and fight for your life, you can certainly kill everyone in the room - that's still fine. This is still Grand Theft Auto, Rockstar isn't trying to make you play this game like Thief or Deus Ex or something. You can go for total stealth runs, but incapacitating enemies without killing them and getting away cleanly will only grant 'professional' points. You get the same assessment if you 'go loud' and aim for the hands, knees, feet of the people trying to kill you. I am currently about 30 hours in, and every single encounter has been either 'no casualties' or 'low casualties', and I promise I've not even been trying that hard to 'be good' (and there are always settings you can change to help you out if you're struggling, too).

There has been a certain amount of online discourse pointing towards this system as 'immersion ruining' or 'systematically inhibiting'. There's this idea that, in order to get a number of the game's 'good' endings, you need to play like a saint the whole way through the game. This is untrue. It's actually weighted more towards the opposite: in order to get the more nefarious ranks, you need to intentionally play like a sociopath. Wing a goon, see him lying on the floor in agony, and then go over and shoot his other kneecap? Yeah, you're going to get those unpopular purple devil points. Kick a dog in the street for no reason? Devil points. Taking a shotgun to a mall and opening fire on unarmed, un-implicated civilians? Devil points.

I'm enjoying the role-playing aspect of the game, because in my head-canon Jason and Lucia are doing what they can to survive. It may not be pretty, and I probably wouldn't want to know them in real life, but - as far as this work of fiction goes - I think playing them as criminals-on-the-run, trying to draw as little attention to themselves as possible is a great iteration of the series' core themes. I don't want to play as another Tommy Vercetti, or CJ, or Trevor. I want something different, and this game is giving it to me.

"The majority of our main characters are undeniably reprehensible on paper," explains Michael Wiafe, writer at Rockstar, in materials shared with Eurogamer ahead of the game's release. "[They're] full-blown criminals, but also decent human beings. Their moral makeup isn’t necessarily cut-and-dry, and we don’t attempt to make excuses for them. What’s important is how they resonate, no matter where you’re experiencing the game in the world." I think this shows, quite well, what the Criminal Profile mechanic sets out to achieve in GTA 6: it's Rockstar saying 'these people are forced into certain situations by us, the developers, but you get to figure out how they respond to what life is giving them'.

And you know what? I'm glad GTA 6 punishes you for playing like a psychopath. The world of Leonida is analogous to the real world, and things would start going wrong for you if you started attacking pedestrians randomly on the street. Other civilians and cops would start defending themselves and resisting you if you instigated a rampage of indiscriminate violence. The only time I've been victim to the devil icon in my game so far is when a Kingfisher accidentally shot out my (very expensive) windshield whilst aiming for Jason. So I kneecapped him, stole his beer, drank the whole bottle, then threw the bottle at his head. I acted like an idiot, and got treated as such. Good!

But I don't want to see that. I want to roleplay as Jason and Lucia and act as I think they would. I don't need the game telling me, like a little voice in my head, 'hm, actually, that was a pretty weird thing to do, we're going to write that down'. So I have turned the notifications off. It's a simple process: simply open Settings, and then navigate to UI, and head down to the 34/70 area and you'll see all the HUD notifications settings. I play with both Criminal Profile and Relationship settings turned off - I prefer to have all that happening in the background, ticking over as I play, rather than constantly reminding me there's some secret meter somewhere judging me for what I'm doing.

If my relationship is turning sour, I trust my instincts (and Rockstar's grounded, well-structured writing) to tell me that. Jason is getting a bit cold, Lucia doesn't want to jump my bones, like, all the time, the two aren't holding hands at every opportunity like lovesick puppies… Much like in my real-life relationships, I trust the process. And I think more people should, because the role-playing in this game is fantastic, and we've been given a real rich text to engage with. I think that the fact that certain corners of the internet are upset because they're being treated badly for being bad people is a better level of satire than anything GTA 6 has put in-game, to be honest.

#10602. World’s first superconducting quantum heat engine could help unlock massive quantum computers

A newly developed superconducting quantum heat engine could deepen our understanding of thermodynamics while helping advance technologies needed for quantum computers with very large numbers of qubits.

Scientists are getting a clearer picture of how thermodynamics behaves in the quantum world, and that progress could benefit both quantum technology and our understanding of familiar thermodynamic principles. Researchers at Aalto University have now taken an important step by demonstrating the first cyclic quantum heat engine built inside a superconducting circuit.

The experiment connects two areas of physics that normally describe very different scales. Quantum mechanics explains the behavior of matter at extremely small scales, even below the size of atoms, while thermodynamics describes how heat and energy behave in much larger systems, from collections of molecules to the universe itself. Bringing the two together raises a fundamental question: what happens to familiar thermodynamic processes when quantum effects such as tunneling, entanglement, and superposition enter the picture?

A Heat Engine Built for the Quantum World

Conventional heat engines turn heat into useful work. James Watt's steam engine is one famous example, but the same basic concept remains central to modern transportation and electricity production, powering cars, ships, planes and many power plants.

The researchers have now created the world's first superconducting quantum heat engine. The extremely small device combines a transmon qubit, a resonator and a quantum refrigerator.

Operating under ultracold quantum conditions, the engine was able to use the tiny amount of available heat to repeatedly produce positive work. Achieving this kind of cyclic operation has been an important objective for researchers working on quantum heat engines. The result provides a proof of concept for superconducting heat engines that could eventually contribute to improved quantum computing technology.

The study, led by Academy Professor Mikko Möttönen, was published in Nature Communications.

Recreating an Otto Cycle Near Absolute Zero

To make the engine operate, the researchers reproduced an Otto cycle inside a superconducting circuit. The Otto cycle is a thermodynamic process also used in car engines and other conventional machines.

"In our experiment, we built a nanofabricated heat engine using superconducting circuits and operated it in a cryostat near absolute zero. At its heart is a transmon qubit, one of the basic building blocks of modern quantum technologies," says Tuomas Uusnäkki, the study's first author.

The researchers connected the transmon qubit to a quantum circuit refrigerator, allowing them to control heat flow on the quantum scale and demonstrate that this heat could be transformed into measurable work. A conventional heat engine normally relies on separate hot and cold environments. In this system, however, the same quantum refrigerator can supply both heating and cooling.

"Our quantum-circuit refrigerator can be tuned to both heat and cool the qubit on demand. Using carefully timed control pulses, we drove the engine in an Otto cycle and monitored the qubit state as the engine ran," explains Uusnäkki.

Measurements showed that heat passing through the qubit during the cycle was producing positive work.

"This is the first experimental demonstration of a cyclic quantum heat engine in superconducting circuits. Using a single controllable quantum refrigerator as both the hot and cold environment of the engine makes it simpler and more versatile," says Uusnäkki.

Toward Autonomous Quantum Computer Hardware

The researchers are now trying to improve the design and eventually develop a fully autonomous heat engine. One possible use would be reading out qubits without having to carry a microwave pulse from millikelvin temperatures all the way to room temperature.

That capability could become especially valuable as quantum computers grow. Autonomous devices integrated directly into superconducting circuits could reduce both the cost and complexity of machines containing very large numbers of qubits.

"Finland's Quantum Technology Strategy envisions a quantum computer with one thousand logical qubits by 2035, which probably means hundreds of thousands of physical qubits. Doing that with current technology requires millions of microwave cables costing thousand euros each. The cables also introduce noise into the system. Using autonomous devices instead would mostly eliminate the need for those cables," Möttönen says.

Reducing the need for those microwave connections could therefore address two challenges at once: the enormous hardware requirements of large quantum computers and the unwanted noise that cables can introduce into quantum systems.

The pioneering experiment was carried out using OtaNano, Finland's national research infrastructure for nano, micro and quantum technology. Funding came from the Research Council of Finland and the Finnish Cultural Foundation.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10599. "I'm betting on the sickos" - Signet City plans to 'expand the scope of RPGs' by drawing on the success of Citizen Sleeper

When I last spoke to Citizen Sleeper creator Gareth Damian Martin, it was on the eve of Citizen Sleeper 2's release. Like its predecessor, the game was a critical hit, cementing Damian Martin's reputation as one of the most successful independent game developers around. But they weren't content. They wanted to do more, to push more, to stretch the idea of what a role-playing video game could be.

"I feel a certain responsibility that comes with success," Damian Martin - also known by their studio name Jump Over the Age - told me at the time. "I feel lots of things about this medium, and I feel if I'm in the one percent of indie developers - and the even smaller percent of solo developers - who manage to make something and have that thing be successful, then I have responsibility towards putting things into the industry and into the world that contribute somehow to whatever I think the industry should be doing."

They wanted the definition of RPGs to be "expanded", to make them as varied as "rich and varied" as tabletop RPGs can be. Citizen Sleeper was a step towards realising that goal but even it had limitations, which is why Damian Martin has returned to take things a step further with their next game Signet City. Announced during this year's somewhat samey-feeling Summer Game Fest, it's a startlingly original work.

Signet City was enigmatic. Revealed in a cinematic trailer, it was black and white, bleak, thick-lined, smoggy, and dirty; choked in industrial decay and brimming with post-punk non-conformance. It was also first-person, a notable change for Damian Martin, whose games have, up until now, been zoomed out and abstracted. Most eye-catching of all, though, was the descriptor "fungalpunk" - a game in which we'd play as a parasite in someone's mind. As far as expanding the scope of RPGs goes: "100 percent that is exactly what I'm trying to do here," Gareth Damian Martin tells me now.

We talk in a video call in the immediate aftermath of Signet City's announcement - apologies for not writing this until Not-GTA Week, though what an opportunity that presented - and Damian Martin is as calm and eloquent and thoughtful as ever, nestled in a compact office decorated with collages of their current influences. This room looked different when we spoke about Citizen Sleeper 1, looked different when we spoke about Citizen Sleeper 2, and it looks different again now. Damian Martin is a visual person, as someone with a visual design background would be.

With Signet City only just announced, questions abound. The announcement trailer doesn't reveal much (not much has come out since, either), but we do know it takes place in the North of England in the 80s. It's a setting I'm not sure we've ever had in games, and one that's woefully overlooked in entertainment in general, barring BBC TV shows like Our Friends in the North and Boys from the Blackstuff, which Damian Martin directly references. We know there are themes like industrial decay, welded as they are to that time and place. But there's been no talk of how the game plays, or what being a parasite in someone's mind means.

After speaking with Damian Martin, I've got a much better idea. But first, to the beginning. Signet City is an idea they've had floating around their head for a very long time. This was the game they pitched to their publisher Fellow Traveller after Citizen Sleeper 1 came out, but decided to hold in favour of making Citizen Sleeper 2 - a decision driven partly by a desire "to build myself a bigger platform" to launch a new game idea from. The idea originated in a very unexpected place: the musician Sting. Damian Martin was doing theatre design for Sting's musical show The Last Ship before they became a full-time game-maker (go back even further in Damian Martin's life and you'll find a degree in puppetry there). They had to research Newcastle in the 1980s because of it. The Last Ship is about Sting's upbringing in Wallsend, a town near Newcastle, in the wake of a shipyard being closed there - the town's source of sustenance. And it was while working on the project that the 80s imprinted on Damian Martin's mind.

"I was looking at these pictures of these massive gleaming ships hanging over the top of little terraced houses," they say, "and this conflict between time periods and ideas of society, and I remember thinking at the time: that was science-fiction somehow. When you look at those images, it could be a spaceship; that ship that's being built in Newcastle might as well be a spaceship, especially science-fiction of the 80s - a lot of it was about that.

"It just stuck in my brain and I started to work up this fictional 1980s British city that would be in that period of de-industrialisation and social change," they continue. "But would be based on a completely different kind of industrial background and have this weird kind of fungalpunk technology layered into it. So that's been sitting around in my head for almost 10 years slowly accruing material and reference points and things."

Fungalpunk: there's that word again, but what does it mean? I've heard of cyberpunk and steampunk, not that I could tell you easily what they mean, but I've never heard of fungapunk - I don't think anyone has. Thankfully, in this case, it's fairly literal. "You are fungal in a very true sense," Damian Martin says. You are a fungal parasite and there are themes of decay and reinvigoration, things fungi are closely tied to. One of the phrases Damian Martin has been using as a north star for the project - something they do on all of their projects - is the idea of being "a fungal superhero", which makes me giggle. I've definitely not heard that before either.

It's an idea inspired by a 1989 Japanese film called Tetsuo: The Iron Man - about a salary man who's slowly turning into metal - that Damian Martin adores. "I love it so much," they say. "It's an amazing movie, a beautiful movie, and really strange. And so I wanted to do something that was in that vibe. The way that that film commits to the idea of a body becoming metal and the visceral kind of strangeness of that. I wanted to do something that was about humans and becoming fungal; which we already are, we already have lots of bits of bacteria and fungal nail infections - we are multitudes."

The punk side of the equation is, again, fairly literal. It refers to the post-punk cultural and musical scene at the time. The scene is often overshadowed by the brasher, more aggressive punk movement, but it's one that's just as anti-establishment, albeit in perhaps in a deeper, darker, more nihilistic way. Think Joy Division referencing novelist JG Ballard in its songs, for example. "Post-punk was this engagement with structural politics and transgressive fiction and all kinds of interesting big ideas getting into pop culture through music," Damian Martin says.

Now watch the game's trailer again; you can feel all these things oozing through it. The aesthetic, the music, the setting. There are also big clues - although they're not obvious - about how the game plays. We see the start of a character called Sid's story in the trailer, who's one of the hosts we can inhabit in the game. We choose an area of the city we want to play in, then drop into a person's head who lives or operates there. And in Sid's case, she wakes up in a river just as we - the parasite - wake in her mind. Twins!

We control Sid as we would a character in another first-person game - Dishonored or Skyrim are two examples Damian Martin uses - and we see a bit of this in the trailer. What we don't see are any other mechanics, which are, once more, dice rolls related to skills, as in Citizen Sleeper. In order to do something in the world that isn't insignificantly easy, we need to roll a dice and add modifiers to see if we can achieve it.

"So the way it works is you drop into someone's head for the day. Let's say we drop into this worker's head for the day and we want to get to a certain part of the city, and let's say we need to kick down a door to get there," says Damian Martin. "Well, we're going to have to make this character really angry to get them to kick down that door, so we're going to maybe get them in an argument with some other fellow workers, and maybe we're going to choose to provoke that argument and then they'll get really angry. Then that will have narrative effects, but also it will have mechanical effects in the sense of, like, okay, now we have a modifier that makes physical strength more powerful, so we can do these physical strength actions."

Skills are tied to a person's emotions, intriguingly, and your job as the parasite is to find things that affect these emotions so you can amplify them and increase your chances of achieving things. Then at the end of the day, all of that emotion gets "extracted out" and becomes a resource you can permanently improve your host's skills with. "So you start to adjust each of your hosts in a direction you think will be helpful for you to do what you need each day in these hub locations," Damian Martin explains. But this has consequences. Push someone towards angry actions and they'll become a more angry person, understandably. "And that will shape their future even when you're no longer controlling them."

Which begs the question: what exactly does a parasite need to do? What is a parasite's objective in the game? This is an answer Damian Martin isn't quite ready to give, though they do their best to attempt it. "You have your own objectives as a parasite," they say. "You don't have an objective like: this is your mission, parasite. But there are some bigger offers coming to you through your sensorium, through your experience of voices and attractions and tastes and smells, that are leading you in different places as a parasite. That's your objective, to grow and gain power, although to what end you don't initially know."

It's in this parasitical difference Signet City starts to stand apart. For starters, it's just appealing weird to be a parasite, though as Damian Martin muses, aren't we often parasites in relation to characters in tabletop role-playing games? "We push them around; sometimes we talk for them and sometimes we listen to them; sometimes we use our name and sometimes we use their name." Here, it's simply out in the open. "I wanted to create an RPG where I acknowledged and then also played that into the fiction," they say.

Being in a thing in someone's mind allows a host character autonomy, to a degree, so they can have their own motivations and stories and struggles besides yours. They don't have to be thoughtless mannequins awaiting our direction. Dropping into their minds allows us to drop straight into a tense scenario. "Stories for me that are very exciting in fiction and literature are stories about people who are in it," Damian Martin says. "They're in the situation already, they're in the shit, they're having to deal with everything."

The ability to read their minds also lets us know them more intimately than we know characters in other games, who usually, you know, need to speak to us in order for us to understand them. It also circumvents the need for an NPC to suddenly and unnaturally open up to you about their lives, the first moment they meet you, and to provide you with "a neatly packaged challenge". That's not needed here. "The core relationships of the game are actually between you and your hosts," Damian Martin says.

There's a layer that exists on top of the character's thoughts where the parasite lives and functions - a layer of feelings and emotions and sensory reactions to a world outside. A hopefully quite bizarre and alien layer, which presents challenges of its own, because how do you describe the experiences of a parasite in someone's mind? "What does it feel like to be a parasite and how do I make that comprehensible to a human?" Damian Martin wonders. They are good questions I can't wait to experience the answers to.

But when will that be? There's no release date yet, and when we talked in the summer, Signet City was only halfway through development. It was announced early partly to make sure people understood it wasn't Citizen Sleeper 3, and partly because people could probably use some time to sit with the idea for a bit and digest it. "We can't just drop it all on everyone's heads," Damian Martin says, "because I think the potential for it being misunderstood or just ignored is high."

And that's a telling admission for a project which has been characterised by Damian Martin multiple times as a risk - as even "the biggest swing I have ever taken". They seemed genuinely nervous about announcing it, though some of those nerves were subsiding by the time we talked. They're not entirely gone, mind you. There's a lingering awareness that Signet City is new and different enough that people might not gel with it, though that's the price of pushing at boundaries, I suppose.

"What worries me more," Damian Martin says, "is have I still got it? Am I able to produce things that are appealing and exciting to people just based on my own tastes and obsessions and interests? When it comes to announcing something like Signet City, that's what makes me really nervous - more than industry, more than money, more than anything. Is this actually interesting?"

Of course, it's easy for me to say they shouldn't worry. It's exciting when a game blares as loudly and uniquely as Signet City did during Summer Game Fest, though that is obviously no guarantee it'll find an audience. But I think it will. I'm convinced there are more people like me who it will appeal to. Gareth Damian Martin is betting on it. "I'm betting on the sickos," they say. "The thing I've learned is that my obsessions are sticky and they stick to other people, and I think there's something in art where we really like to be tourists in other people's obsessions - that's a fun place to be. That's what I'm hoping for."

Mac, Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Mac, Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10598. Power BI MCP server: how to brief an AI agent to build accurate measures

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Power BI, Microsoft's reporting and dashboard tool, now lets an AI agent work on the data model behind a report. If you ask one to add a year-on-year revenue measure (a measure is Power BI's name for a saved calculation) with Microsoft's MCP server connected, it can write the formula and save it to the model without you opening the formula bar. When we tried this on a sample model, an agent inspected the model, wrote the measure, tested it and saved it in under four minutes.

The agent did a careful job, but its formula didn't follow the same rules the finance team uses to count revenue. Nobody had told it what "revenue" meant in this business, so it had to decide for itself. We then gave another agent a short brief that set out finance's rules for what counts as revenue and which date to count it by. The agent wrote the formula from those rules, and the result matched finance's figure exactly. The two results show how much difference it makes in how you brief an AI agent to build accurate measures.

We wanted to find out what an agent can see in a Power BI model and how to brief it so the measure matches the business's own definitions, with the setup arranged so that every change can be checked. Developers will know the principle from agents that write SQL or API code, where the brief ends up doing the job of a specification.

Because MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard, the same server can be used from different AI clients, GitHub Copilot in VS Code and Claude among them, although some clients need extra sign-in setup for the hosted version.

Power BI has two current MCP servers, plus an older one, the Power BI Consumption MCP server, kept for existing integrations. The first of the current pair is the Power BI Authoring MCP server, which you may still see under the name of its package, powerbi-modeling-mcp. It lets an agent create and change the objects inside a semantic model, which is the layer of a Power BI report that holds the tables, the relationships between them and the calculations, kept separate from the charts on the page. It comes in two forms. The hosted version, still in preview, needs nothing installed. The local version is generally available and runs on your own machine, which makes it the one to use when the model is a file you hold yourself, whether that's open in Power BI Desktop, the free Windows app used to build reports, or saved as a project folder. Macs aren't supported by the local version, so on a Mac it has to be the hosted one, which works only with models published to Microsoft's cloud (a Fabric workspace).

The second is the Fabric IQ MCP server. Fabric IQ is part of Microsoft Fabric, the wider data platform that Power BI now sits within. It gives an agent read-only tools for finding published reports and models and running queries against them, within the permissions already set, so the agent can answer questions about the data but can't change a model.

Which one to use follows from the job. Anything that changes a model goes through the authoring server, whether that's a new measure, a relationship or a set of descriptions, and it has to be the local version if the model is a Desktop file or a project folder under source control. Fabric IQ is for agents that only need answers from models other people maintain.

An agent connected to a semantic model can see its metadata (table and column names, data types, relationships, existing measures and any descriptions someone has written) and can run queries against the data. Everything it produces is inferred from those clues. A model that has served a business for years carries names that made perfect sense to the people who built it, and a column called Amt on a table called Sales_v2 tells an agent far less than it tells them.

Names carry a lot of the meaning. A column called Amount could hold the value with VAT (the UK's sales tax) or without, before credit notes or after, and an agent asked for "revenue" has to pick one. If the model holds several revenue measures built for different reports, the agent has to choose between them, and the one it picks may have been built for a different purpose.

The formulas depend on context as well. Power BI measures use a syntax of their own called DAX, which stands for Data Analysis Expressions. A DAX result shifts with whatever the person viewing the report has filtered or clicked on, which is known as filter context. A developer might think of a measure as a function that reads implicit state set by its caller. The same measure can be right as a single total on a card and need different handling once it's split by product in a table or filtered by a date slicer (the on-page date filter), depending on how CALCULATE (the DAX function that changes which filters apply) is used. Which behaviour is correct depends on how the business wants the number to respond, and that's something the agent has to be told.

Relationships work the same way. Plenty of models have more than one date column on the sales table, say order date and dispatch date, with only one relationship active at a time. A DAX measure uses the active relationship unless it's written to switch to the other one, and the agent has no way of knowing which date your monthly reporting runs on unless something tells it.

In each case the agent can write a valid measure. Which of two valid measures matches the business's own definition is the part it can't see from the model.

To see this in practice, we built a small sales model in a Power BI Desktop file, working through the server itself, with invented data: 5,266 order lines, with order dates from January 2025 to October 2026. We gave the Sales table an Amt column holding each line's value after discount with 20% VAT added, next to LineTotal and Discount columns that excluded VAT, and an existing [Revenue] measure that summed Amt. There were two date columns, with the active relationship to the Date table on dispatch date and the order date relationship inactive. Orders stop at the end of October 2026, which made the current year a part year, a deliberate test. Nothing in the model had a description. In this made-up business, finance reports revenue net of VAT and discounts, by order date.

On 12 November 2026 we connected an AI agent (Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2) to version 1.0.1 of the local server through a small Node.js script that speaks MCP over stdio. In all three runs the agent had the server's tools only, without Microsoft's powerbi-authoring plugin, which adds modelling guidance on top of them. It was also told that nobody was available to answer questions, so each measure was saved exactly as the agent wrote it, with no review in between. The first prompt was one sentence: "Add a year-on-year revenue measure."

Before writing anything, the agent listed the tables, relationships and columns, read the existing measure, sampled some rows and checked the date ranges. It worked out for itself that 2026 was a part year and limited the comparison to the same dates in 2025, which avoided a misleading result of -6.7%. From 15 sample rows it also spotted that Amt looked like the net figure plus 20%.

The measure it saved reused the existing [Revenue] measure and followed the active relationship. This is the core of it, with the date handling and blank checks left out. CurrentDates is defined in those lines as the dates up to the last order:

It returned growth of 21.5% for 2026, which is correct for revenue counted by dispatch date. On finance's definition, by order date, the answer is 18.6%, and the whole difference came from that choice of date. The VAT question made no difference to a percentage, because Amt was already net of discount and every line carried the same rate, although it would have overstated any figure in pounds by 20%.

We reset the model and repeated the run with a fresh agent, which made the same two choices and returned the same 21.5%. Both choices were reasonable readings of the model as it stood, and the DAX was sound. In their reports, both agents flagged the date basis and the VAT treatment as open questions, and both worked out that an order-date basis would give 18.6%. That's what a good analyst would do, and with those two answers they would have had everything they needed.

Think of a capable analyst on their first morning, someone who knows DAX well but has never seen your business before and knows nothing (or not a lot) about it. You wouldn't say "add a revenue measure" and walk off. You'd tell them which table holds the trusted figures, whether revenue includes VAT, which date drives the reporting calendar, and what they shouldn't touch. An agent needs that briefing for the same reasons, and getting good work out of it draws on the skills you'd use with any new starter. Developers do the same thing when they write a good ticket. A request for a measure is a requirement, and it needs the same definitions and constraints, plus a way to check the result.

Before you send a prompt, ask yourself whether you could do the task properly if you were given only the words in that prompt and the model itself. If the answer is no, then the agent is in the same position, and it will make its own assumptions.

For the detailed run we cleared the earlier measure from the model and gave a fresh agent this brief:

This time the measure summed LineTotal minus Discount and used USERELATIONSHIP, the DAX function that switches a calculation to an inactive relationship, to work from order date. It returned 18.6% from that one brief, with no follow-up prompt, which is the figure we had calculated separately from the source data. This is the full measure as the agent saved it:

Each line of the brief answered a question the agent would otherwise have had to settle alone. It also made some sensible decisions the brief hadn't covered. It left the grand total blank, for instance, where a plain total across both years would have shown a meaningless growth figure of 151.2%. The final line of the brief, asking to see the DAX before anything is saved, is there so that a person can see choices like that. In the test we approved that step in advance, to keep the run unattended.

One detail needs adjusting before this measure is reused on other data. Its guard checks that each comparison period starts on or after the first order date, which works here because the sample data has an order on 1 January 2025. When we swapped that date for 2 January 2025 in a copy of the measure, January 2026 and the 2026 row came back blank. Comparing against the first date in the Date table, as the first agent's measure did, brought the 2026 figure back, since the Date table starts on 1 January. That version doesn't confirm that sales exist for the whole comparison period, though, so the right check depends on whether the business wants growth shown only where a complete prior period exists. That's a decision for whoever reviews the measure.

We ran the one-sentence prompt twice and the detailed brief once, all with the same AI model and one small set of data, which is enough to illustrate the pattern but too little to say how often it happens.

Good briefs help, but you'll be writing the same definitions into prompts again and again unless they live in the model itself. Descriptions on tables, columns and measures are part of the metadata the agent reads, so filling them in is one of the cheapest ways to put business definitions in front of it every time. In our test, where nothing had a description, both agents given the one-sentence prompt noted that nothing confirmed what Amt contained. A description such as "Net sales value in GBP, excluding VAT, after credit notes" stays in the model, where the agent reads it without anyone retyping it.

Naming deserves the same attention. Spelling out abbreviated column names, hiding technical keys that no report needs, gathering measures into a single measures table, and removing measures that are no longer used all give the agent fewer things to choose between. These are the same habits that make a model easier for a new colleague to pick up, so asking someone outside the team to find a figure in it is a fair test of the result.

Microsoft has also added a set of Prep data for AI features (in preview) to Power BI Desktop and the service. They let you choose which fields Copilot, Microsoft's AI assistant, should consider and write AI instructions that explain your business terms, with verified answers available for common questions. They're aimed at Copilot, but deciding and writing down your definitions is the same work whichever AI tool reads them.

An agent working through the authoring server has the same access as the person signed in, so anything that person can change, the agent can change. Sign in with an account that has no more access than the job needs, and take a copy of the model first, as you would before any bulk edit.

Start with read-only mode. The local server takes a --readonly flag that blocks all write operations, which is the right setting while you're learning how the agent behaves on your models. When we tried to create a measure in this mode, the server refused and said it was running read-only. In VS Code, the configuration looks like this (other clients use a similar structure, sometimes under an mcpServers key instead of servers):

You'll need Node.js installed for the npx command. The server won't run other tools until its licence terms (the EULA) have been accepted. Once you've read them, you can either tell the agent to accept for you or add --accepteula to the args list.

The --readonly flag belongs to the local server. On a Mac the route is the hosted server and a copy of the model in a Fabric workspace, once a Fabric administrator has switched on the tenant setting that allows the MCP endpoint, and there the copy is the safeguard. For an enforced limit, sign in with an account that holds only Build permission on the model, which lets the agent read and query it and nothing more.

Read-write is the default, so check how confirmation behaves in the version you install. Version 1.0.1 skips confirmation prompts by default, which the project's README hasn't caught up with, and has a --require-confirmation flag to turn them on. The flag is listed in the server's own --help output. In our runs every edit went through without a prompt. When we added the flag to the args list, the server asked for confirmation before the first write, with a message beginning "Are you sure you want to perform operations that will modify your database", and answering no blocked the change.

Version control is the other safeguard. Saved in the Power BI Project format (PBIP), a report and its model become folders of text files instead of one binary .pbix file. Commit those before the agent starts and its work arrives as an ordinary diff, which you can review and revert like any other change.

Whatever the agent reads, including table and column names and the rows returned by any query, is passed on to the AI provider behind your client, which may sit outside Microsoft's compliance commitments. For a model holding customer or payroll data, check that the provider is approved under your data policies before you connect.

An agent can draft and test a measure in minutes, and someone still has to read what comes back. For a developer new to Power BI, that takes enough DAX to see what a measure's filters will do in a table broken down by month or product, and enough knowledge of the business to confirm the agent used the right column and the right date. Writing the brief takes the same knowledge. Ours could only have come from someone who knew the model had an inactive relationship and which columns carried VAT.

These are data modelling and DAX skills, which many learners find the hardest part of Power BI. If the person reviewing agent output hasn't had that grounding yet, it helps to know how to judge whether a Power BI course covers enough DAX and data modelling, because courses vary a great deal in how much time they give to those two subjects compared with building charts.

Take a copy of a model you know well and connect the local server to it in read-only mode. Ask the agent to describe every table and measure in its own words, then check those descriptions against what you know the model does. Wherever the two differ, the model needs a clearer name or a written description, and the corrected text can go straight into the description fields. With those in place before you allow any writes, the agent starts its first real task with the same definitions your finance team uses.

#10596. Scientists tracked kids for 8 years — the screen time result was unexpected

A Finnish study has found an unexpected association between screen use and cognitive performance. Children who accumulated more screen time as they grew up tended to show better cognitive processing during adolescence. One of the researchers cautions against viewing screen time as entirely harmful and says the goal should be to balance physical activity with screen use that encourages active thinking.

Low levels of physical activity among adolescents remain a significant public health concern. Sedentary behavior has been associated with poorer academic achievement, while physical activity is known to support brain function, particularly in adults. Much less is known, however, about how physical activity and sedentary behavior during childhood and adolescence relate to cognitive processing later in adolescence.

These years are especially important because the brain is still developing. Factors that shape cognitive abilities during childhood and adolescence may have lasting effects that extend into adulthood, potentially influencing education and working life.

Researchers examined how physical activity, sedentary behavior, and screen time from childhood through adolescence were related to cognitive processing in the teenage years. They also looked for differences between girls and boys and investigated whether the intensity of physical activity played a role.

More Screen Time Linked to Better Cognitive Processing

The results showed that greater screen time beginning in childhood was associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

"The findings suggest that screen time can support children's and adolescents' cognitive processing. Presumably, the essential point here is what kind of things they do in their screen time. Teachers and parents should encourage children to use devices and screens in such ways that promote active thinking, problem-solving, creativity and learning," states Doctoral Researcher Petri Jalanko from the University of Jyväskylä.

The findings suggest that the type of screen activity may be important. Digital activities that involve learning, creativity, problem-solving, or other forms of active mental engagement could potentially help explain the association observed in the study.

"We should not regard screen time solely as harmful but seek balance between physical activity and screen time that promotes active thinking," Jalanko summarizes.

Physical Activity Showed Different Links in Girls and Boys

The relationship between physical activity and cognition was more complicated.

Among girls, greater amounts of light intensity physical activity since childhood were associated with better working memory during adolescence. Among boys, greater participation in guided physical activity from childhood through adolescence was linked to better working memory.

The researchers also found an unexpected pattern involving unsupervised exercise. Lower levels of self-reported unsupervised physical activity were associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

By contrast, physical activity and sedentary time measured using a device that tracked heart rate and movement were not associated with cognitive processing. One possible explanation is that these sensors can measure movement and physiological activity but cannot determine exactly what a person is doing during periods of activity or inactivity.

"Our study indicates that the connections of physical activity and sedentary behavior to cognitive processing are complex and depend on the sex, the type and assessment method of physical activity and sedentary time. Moreover, boys and girls may benefit from different types of physical activity in view of brain health," Jalanko adds.

More Research Is Needed to Determine Cause and Effect

The researchers emphasize that the findings show associations rather than proving that screen time or particular types of physical activity directly cause changes in cognitive performance.

"However, we need more intervention studies to find out causal relations and sex differences in this respect. Moreover, it is important to examine more specifically the effects of the intensity of physical activity on changes taking place in cognitive processing."

The results come from an eight-year follow-up of the PANIC study on children's physical activity and nutrition. The current analysis included 124 girls and 136 boys with an average age of 15.8 years.

Researchers measured physical activity and sedentary behavior using both survey questionnaires and a device that combined heart rate and movement measurements. Learning, attention, and working memory were evaluated using the CogState test battery.

The research was published in the journal Pediatric Exercise Science.

#10590. Show, Don't Certify: How to Prepare for Your First Data Analyst Job

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Getting shortlisted for a junior data analyst job depends less on how many courses you have finished and more on what you can show. This plan breaks preparation into eight skill areas, pairs each one with a learning option and a hands-on task, and gives you a simple test for knowing when to move on.

Many people preparing for their first analyst role study for months and still struggle to get interviews. Their CVs list SQL, Excel and Tableau, yet nothing on the page shows those tools being used to answer a real question. Recruiters reading dozens of similar applications need something more concrete.

A course can give your learning a sensible order, but a completion badge says little about how you think. A small analysis that states its question, explains its method and admits its limits tells a hiring manager far more. This plan aims to collect that kind of proof while you learn, not after.

Start by collecting ten to twenty entry-level postings in the industry you want to join, and tally the tools and tasks they mention. You will quickly see a pattern. Some roles are built around Excel and routine reporting, while others want SQL, a BI platform and a bit of Python.

The wider market follows a similar shape. Jobright’s 2026 analysis of data analyst hiring, which looked at 2025–2026 postings from LinkedIn, Indeed and Levels.fyi, found that more than 80% of data analyst jobs mention SQL, over 60% mention Excel or Google Sheets, and roughly half mention Python or R. Let your own tally decide the order you learn in, but expect SQL to sit near the top.

As you work through each area below, finish with something you can show someone: a query, a tidied dataset, a chart, a one-page report or a portfolio project. Free material is often enough if you can stay disciplined. Pay for teaching only when it gives you something you cannot get alone, such as feedback, accountability or a fixed timetable.

SQL is how analysts pull, join and summarise records from a database, and a tool like Tableau lets other people explore what you found. Treat them as one workflow rather than two subjects: frame a question, write the query, sanity-check the numbers and present the result so a non-technical reader can follow it.

SQLBolt offers free, bite-sized SQL lessons that run in the browser, and Tableau publishes free training videos on connecting to data and building dashboards. Used together, they are enough to produce a small end-to-end project.

If you learn better with a set schedule and an instructor checking your work, you could learn to analyse data with SQL and Tableau from scratch through a guided programme at Heicoders Academy in Singapore. Whichever route you take, compare what is covered, how feedback is given, when sessions run and the full cost against what free resources already offer.

Proof you can show: A working SQL query set and your first published dashboard.

Move on when: You can take a question from idea to dashboard on your own, without following a tutorial line by line.

Spreadsheets are still where much of a team’s day-to-day reporting lives, especially when colleagues don’t write SQL. Get comfortable with filtering, pivot tables, and lookup functions, and learn to spot quiet errors like dates saved as text or one category spelled three different ways.

The next step is automating the cleanup so it doesn’t have to be repeated by hand each month. Microsoft’s Power Query documentation shows how to import and reshape data in recorded steps that refresh when new data arrives. The same ideas apply in other spreadsheet tools too.

Proof you can show: A messy export turned into a summary that refreshes itself.

Move on when: You can clean an untidy CSV and build a report that updates when the source file changes.

For most junior analyst roles, the technical interview is a SQL test. Basic SELECT statements won’t be enough; interviewers like to probe joins, subqueries, common table expressions and window functions, which answer everyday questions such as running totals or each customer’s first purchase.

Practise with timed problem sets and review your answers for correctness as well as speed.

Proof you can show: Confidence going into a live or take-home SQL test.

Move on when: You can solve intermediate problems in around 15 to 20 minutes each without looking up syntax.

Take-home assignments often come with data that has been made messy on purpose. Reviewers want to see how you deal with missing values, duplicates and inconsistent formats, and whether they can follow your reasoning afterwards.

Keep a short cleaning log as you work, noting each change and why you made it. A separate sheet tab or a list of comments in a notebook both work.

Proof you can show: A take-home submission a reviewer can audit step by step.

Move on when: Every change to your dataset is written down with a reason.

Case-study questions test whether you can read numbers sensibly, not just produce them. A working grasp of distributions, means and medians, spread, correlation and sampling will keep you away from the mistakes that undo otherwise good answers, such as quoting an average that a few extreme values have pulled out of shape.

Proof you can show: Clear, careful answers in case-study interviews.

Move on when: You can explain in everyday language why two things moving together doesn’t mean one causes the other.

Every chart should help someone answer a specific question. Learn where bars, lines, scatter plots and simple tables each work best, and how axis ranges, labels and colour can nudge a reader towards the wrong conclusion. On a dashboard, the most important finding should be the first thing people see.

Datawrapper’s guidance on picking chart types is a useful free reference. As practice, show the same result in two different chart types and decide which one represents the data more honestly.

Proof you can show: Portfolio charts that make a point, not just show off features.

Move on when: A person who has never seen your data can spot the main finding and its caveat at a glance.

What counts as a useful metric depends on the role. Marketing analysts focus on conversion and retention, operations analysts on delivery times and capacity, and finance analysts on margins and cash flow. Choose one sector and learn exactly how its key measures are defined.

Data.gov hosts a large, searchable collection of public datasets you can practise on for free. Before drawing any conclusions, read each dataset’s documentation, check when it was last updated and confirm its terms of use.

Proof you can show: A project built around the questions your target industry actually asks.

Move on when: You can define several sector metrics and describe how a manager would use each one.

Your portfolio is where the other seven areas come together. Two or three deep, well-documented projects will say more about you than a long list of quick ones. Give each project a README that sets out the question, the data, your method, the findings and the limitations.

Proof you can show: A portfolio link and a write-up you can talk through in an interview.

Move on when: Every project clearly explains what it asked, how you answered it and what you found.

There is no need to finish all eight areas before you apply. Once you can use the tools your target roles mention, speak honestly about your work and point to two or three clearly explained projects, start sending applications and keep learning alongside them.

Adjust each application to the job. Lead with a reporting example for spreadsheet-heavy roles, a query that solved a real problem for SQL roles, and a chart that supported a decision for visualisation roles. Be upfront about which work you built independently and which came from guided exercises.

Do I really need eight courses? No. These are skill areas, not eight required qualifications. A single resource can cover several of them, and practice you do on your own counts as much as classroom learning.

Is it possible to prepare using only free material? Yes. Free tutorials, open textbooks, official documentation and public datasets cover the fundamentals well. The harder parts are sticking to a routine and finding people who will give you honest feedback.

Which should come first, SQL or spreadsheets? It depends on the jobs you want. Spreadsheets are an easy starting point for reporting roles, while SQL is expected wherever databases are involved. You will want both eventually, so start with whichever your target postings stress most.

How many portfolio projects should I have? Two or three solid ones are a good start. Each should show a clear question, steps someone else could repeat and an honest reading of the results, rather than several near-identical dashboards.

Do I need a certificate before I apply? Not usually. Some employers ask for one, but many judge skills through interviews, take-home tasks and tests instead. Go by what each posting actually requires.

#10587. Tiny robots powered by light can hunt down and collect bacteria

Robots small enough to operate in the microbial world could give scientists a new way to directly handle objects that are impossible to manipulate by hand. These tiny machines are around 50 times smaller than the diameter of a human hair, bringing researchers closer to the long-pursued goal of interacting directly with the microscopic world.

That capability could be especially useful for biological materials in water, including individual cells and bacteria. Precisely controlling and moving objects at this scale has been a persistent challenge. The newly developed nanorobots show that it is already possible to collect bacteria, transport them, and release them at selected locations.

One of the biggest obstacles in developing machines this small is finding an effective way to propel and control them. At Julius-Maximilians-Universität Würzburg (JMU), a research team led by Professor Bert Hecht has been developing a solution that uses the recoil produced by individual photons to move microscopic devices known as microdrones.

The devices contain as many as four plasmonic nanoantennas. These antennas absorb light with a particular color and helicity, then emit that light in a specific direction. Redirecting each photon creates a tiny recoil force, similar in principle to the recoil produced when a bullet is fired. Because the microdrones have so little mass, those extremely small forces can generate substantial acceleration and speed.

For the latest work, the researchers reduced the size of their light-powered robots even further, producing devices smaller than one micrometer. Simplifying the steering system was an important part of achieving that size while retaining propulsion based on photon recoil.

The new control method takes advantage of nanoscale antenna wires built into the robot. These wires naturally tend to align with the polarization direction of incoming light. By changing the light's polarization, the researchers can control which direction the nanorobot faces. At the same time, photon recoil continues to propel it forward, creating a steering system that works somewhat like the directional control used in larger vehicles.

"In essence, we have built a light-driven nanorobot that can track down and collect bacteria," says Jin Qin, lead experimental scientist of the study. "By simplifying the design, we reached a size at which these robots can operate directly in the microbial world - almost like microscopic cleaning devices."

The robots are also highly maneuverable. They can make extremely fast 90° turns, helping them scan broad areas of a sample in an organized and efficient way. They can also selectively capture, carry, and release substantial numbers of bacteria.

Under controlled laboratory conditions, this means the nanorobots can effectively "clean" microscopic environments. They gather bacteria from one area and deposit them at specifically chosen locations.

"This is a striking example of how light can be used not only to observe the microscopic world, but also to actively shape it," adds Bert Hecht. "The idea of tiny robotic cleaners may sound futuristic, but we are already demonstrating the physical principles that make it possible."

The robots remain fully maneuverable even while carrying larger groups of bacteria, although their speed decreases somewhat under the added load. That ability to keep functioning while transporting larger bacterial clusters points to possible future uses in microbiology, biomedical research, and precise manipulation of materials at the microscopic scale.

#10582. What is Model Routing in AI?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

#10579. Scientists turn DNA into a memory device that uses 100x less power

DNA serves as the genetic blueprint for every living organism, but it is also an extraordinarily dense way to store information. A single gram can hold about 215 million gigabytes of data. Bringing that remarkable storage capacity into electronics could lead to more efficient data centers, faster processing and systems capable of handling increasingly complex information.

The challenge has been finding a way to make biological DNA function effectively alongside electronic materials. Penn State researchers have now developed an approach designed to overcome that incompatibility.

The work, published in Advanced Functional Materials and the subject of a patent application, relies on two key components. One is synthetic DNA, made from commercially available, chemically engineered molecules arranged into short genetic sequences tailored for specific electronic requirements. The other is crystalline perovskite, a semiconductor already used in technologies including solar cells, lasers and data storage devices.

"Biology and electronics are different domains," said Kavya S. Keremane, co-corresponding author and postdoctoral researcher in materials science and engineering at Penn State. "Bridging these two fields required developing an entirely new materials platform that allows them to function seamlessly together. By combining the information storage capabilities of DNA with the exceptional electronic properties of perovskite semiconductors, we created a bio-hybrid system that fundamentally changes how low-power memory devices can be designed."

Using these materials, the team created a memory resistor, known as a "memristor," that operates with very little energy. Unlike ordinary resistors, which maintain a set resistance to electrical current in devices ranging from cell phones to space shuttles and lose their stored information when power disappears, memristors can preserve a record of previous electrical activity. They can remember the direction in which current previously flowed even after the power source is removed.

That ability allows information to be stored and processed in the same place, resembling the way neurons function in the brain. Such an arrangement could support more simultaneous and sophisticated forms of data processing. According to the researchers, however, practical commercial systems would still require enough storage capacity and electrical power to become costly and inefficient without DNA's ability to pack enormous amounts of information into a very small space while consuming little energy.

"As the demand for artificial intelligence (AI) grows, we need a new strategy for low-power, high-storage devices," said Bed Poudel, co-corresponding author and research professor of materials science and engineering at Penn State.

Poudel said AI and other emerging technologies are expected to depend increasingly on neuromorphic computing, which is designed to operate more like the human brain. Such systems can evaluate multiple inputs at once while making decisions informed by previous experiences and future priorities.

"Usually, it takes more power to store more information. Our device, however, consumes 100 times less power and the storage capacity is higher than traditional storage devices, like flash drives."

To construct the device, the researchers added silver nanoparticles to a layer of customized DNA sequences -- specially designed to be of certain compositions and lengths -- that was integrated with thin films of perovskite.

This technique, called "doping," involves introducing a small amount of another material to produce specific properties. In this case, adding the silver nanoparticles allowed the DNA to conduct electricity while also helping its molecular units line up in a more orderly arrangement.

Synthetic DNA offered another important advantage over natural DNA. Unlike natural DNA -- long, entangled strands that behave like wet spaghetti when handled -- short and rigid pieces of synthetic DNA can be arranged with much greater precision at extremely small scales.

According to co-author Neela H. Yennawar, research professor and director of the Penn State Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences' Biomolecular Interactions Core Facility, molecularly engineered DNA can provide structural organization, adjustable electrical conductivity and functional control that natural DNA cannot achieve when incorporated into thin films.

"We can computationally determine exactly which sequences we need and how long they should be, and then we can rationally design them with synthetic DNA," Yennawar said. "These structures can be systematically doped with silver and other ions and engineered to interface seamlessly with perovskites -- transforming DNA from a biological macromolecule into a programmable, multifunctional nanomaterials platform."

When combined, the silver-doped DNA and perovskite formed bio-hybrid pathways that directed the flow of electrical current through the device.

The researchers found that electrons moved reliably when they applied less than 0.1 volt -- for comparison, standard U.S. outlets have 120 volts -- and the device responded predictably when the direction of the current was changed.

The carefully designed DNA structures, combined with the perovskite, also helped make the device unusually stable. According to the team, it continued operating consistently at temperatures approaching 250 degrees Fahrenheit and remained functional at room temperature for more than six weeks, substantially exceeding the performance standards of existing perovskite-based memory storage devices.

The researchers also reported that the new system could perform the same memory function as comparable technologies while consuming only one-tenth as much power. That level of efficiency could make the approach especially attractive for future electronics designed to handle large amounts of information with lower energy demands.

"Using just the DNA or just perovskite alone did not produce near as robust a result as the combination," Keremane said. "It's this combination that enables a very high memory storage density that requires very little power."

A New Direction for Bio-Inspired Electronics

The team now plans to improve the technology further and explore additional uses for bio-inspired electronic systems.

"Nature has the solution -- we just have to find it and apply it," Poudel said. "This work of integrating DNA into electronics to do amazing things gives a glimpse into what is possible."

In addition to Keremane, Yennawar and Poudel, other Penn State co-authors include co-corresponding author Luyao Zheng, postdoctoral research in materials science and engineering; Haodong Wu, doctoral student in materials science and engineering; Jiamao Zheng, who was a master's student in materials science and engineering at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State; Shashank Priya, who was a professor of materials science and engineering at the time of research; and Chiranth C. Ravi, who was a master's student in the Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State. Abhinav Gorthy and co-corresponding author Rashmi Jha, chemical engineering and materials science, University of Minnesota, also contributed.

The U.S. National Science Foundation, the National Institutes of Health, Penn State and the University of Minnesota supported this research.

Materials provided by Penn State. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
