#10482. Nvidia proves it’s too big to fail

Good morning. Today I’m deciding whether the final Avengers: Doomsday trailer passes the “people just standing around” test, trying the new Material 3 Expressive Claude app on my Android devices, and stocking up on discounted XTEINK e-readers for Christmas gifts.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Eleven days after leaving Nvidia, Jensen Huang is back as president, CEO, and a board director. The board voted unanimously to reinstate him on Monday, saying it was satisfied with his candor and ability to lead following a series of meetings. It also said input from senior levels of the US government left it confident that his reinstatement poses no national security risk.

I didn’t see this coming. Huang was dishonest to the public and to his board. He admitted to having affairs not only with an alleged Chinese spy, but also with an employee who directly reported to him. He is accused of facilitating the sale of Nvidia AI chips to companies like Megaspeed that knowingly purchased them with the intention of smuggling them into China, a contravention of US trade restrictions.

As recently as November 17th, the board chair, Harvey Jones, reiterated that the company had no intention of bringing back Huang as CEO. But things changed quickly on Sunday as President Trump reportedly reassured the board that Huang’s reinstatement posed no security risk to US intelligence, something that the new Democratic-led Congress will be sure to test via repeated subpoenas in the new year.

As Huang walked into the atrium at Nvidia’s headquarters, staff gathered around to cheer his return, and he addressed the crowd by saying he would be a “better CEO the second time around,” as if 11 days of unemployment had given him sufficient time to evaluate his past behavior.

Nvidia’s stock was up over 30% on Monday, rebounding from a disastrous end to last week that wiped out $4 trillion in Nasdaq value. It’s hard not to see Huang’s return as a direct response to this short-term disruption, which also hinted at a much more serious correction in the stock market. Nvidia’s stock had already fallen 24% between the day Huang resigned and Friday’s crunch, and with looming regulation from Congress in 2027, a rudderless AI industry could have been disastrous for the US economy writ large.

Though things may appear to be back to normal, or as normal as possible given the insanity of the past couple of weeks, Huang has returned to a very different trade environment. While he was away, Nvidia’s board formed a National Security and Export Control Committee to investigate whether the company’s actions violated any US embargoes. And as part of Huang’s return, Nvidia has committed to hiring its first chief compliance officer, an executive who will ostensibly not report directly to the CEO.

I was pretty gobsmacked when I saw the news of Huang’s return come across the wire at 9am ET yesterday. I’m still wrapping my head around it. Is Nvidia too big to fail, or just a symptom of a larger interdependency between the US executive branch and Big Tech? Huang promises to be a better leader this time around, but to whom: his employees, his customers, or his shareholders? Because he appears to be a very different person to each of those groups.

Better-tasting water for your morning coffee, everyday cooking, or a house full of holiday guests? Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday sale has options for all three, from high-capacity under-sink reverse osmosis systems to installation-free countertop models. Upgrade your kitchen with the X12 under-sink system for $899, save space with the compact G6A6 for $189.99, or skip the plumbing with the M6HG countertop system for $299, which delivers filtered hot water for coffee and tea in seconds.

Whichever setup fits your routine, use code 9TO5GBF26 to take an extra 5% off those sale prices. Waterdrop’s offers also extend to whole-home filtration and replacement refrigerator filters, giving you plenty of ways to upgrade without stretching your holiday budget. Shop Waterdrop’s Black Friday deals on Amazon before the sale ends November 30th.

Since it’s Black Friday week, I figured I’d round up some of the best deals we’ve found so far.

A discounted Pixel: The Pixel 10 at $600 is a really good deal. Recent software updates have closed some of the feature gaps with the Pixel 11, and the hardware is still solid even a year after launch. If you want better cameras, the Pixel 11 Pro at $749 is an absolute steal.

The gaming-tablet deal: The Legion Tab Gen 5 at $599 makes much more sense than it did at $899: an 8.8-inch 165Hz display, a 12GB/256GB configuration, and an included folio. It’s a good fit for someone specifically seeking a compact Android gaming machine.

The last-generation watch: The 40mm Galaxy Watch 8 at $199 is the sort of older smartwatch worth considering when annual upgrades are modest. And because the Watch 9 is basically identical, I’d go for the Watch 8. If you are looking at Samsung’s latest and greatest, the Galaxy Watch 9 is $379 with a free $50 Amazon gift card, which is a decent deal, too.

A deal on the year’s best Android foldable: The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is showing up at $1,499.99 for some lucky shoppers and $1,549.99 for the rest. Either way, it’s Samsung’s best product of 2026, and my current favorite gadget.

Google’s latest mid-sized sedan AI model is taking the world by storm. Gemini 4 Neon is technically the middle sibling in Google’s lineup, with Carbon at the top and Hydrogen at the bottom, but everyone seems to agree it absolutely cooks.

If you’ve spent any time on X over the past few days (I hate myself for writing that), you’ll likely have seen an uptick in fervor over Google’s latest model, which seems to outperform Claude Opus 5.5, and even Gemini 4 Carbon itself, in many benchmarks and real-world tests. Even Google is providing some helpful guidance about when to use one or the other.

One thing I’ve read again and again about Neon is how adept it is at writing. I’m of two minds about this: the better an AI gets at doing my job, the less necessary I am at said job. On the other hand, I’m so tired of obvious AI slop that a model that actually writes well sounds like a relief. At least when I’m unemployed, I won’t be yelling at the screen all day about why the robot yet again used negative parallelism.

I didn’t get to play much GTA VI over the weekend, but I know a few (million) people who did. Publisher Take-Two says the game generated $3.79 billion in first-day retail sales. That’s an incredible number in any industry, but a truly mind-blowing one in the video game world. Rockstar also released its first patch for the game on Monday to fix a modest number of bugs with the launch code.

If you follow open-weight AI models, you’ll likely have heard of how influential Z.ai’s GLM-5.2 was when it launched in June. Now the company has released GLM-5.4, and it’s novel as much for its architecture as it is for its benchmarks.

Its Loop Transformer reuses a block of layers to do more computation without adding parameters. The company is pitching applications beyond software, including games, visual work, and CAD. The 750-million-parameter model reportedly performs at the level of a 2-3 trillion parameter non-looping equivalent, but we’re still waiting on a broader release for real-world testing.

In browser news, Ladybird’s first public alpha is now downloadable for macOS and Linux. It’s built with a brand-new engine that eschews many of the legacy decisions made by Google’s Chromium team. And the project has the backing of big names like Shopify, Cloudflare, and Proton. It’s still pretty rough, and features are paltry, but any new competition in the browser space is a win in my book.

#10479. This new qubit could be 100 times less error-prone in superfluid quantum computer breakthrough

Researchers at the University of Surrey have proposed a new kind of qubit that could help address one of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing: keeping error rates low as systems become larger.

The concept uses superfluid helium, a form of helium with unusual quantum properties, to create a qubit that may be less sensitive to some of the disturbances that affect today's leading quantum computers.

Why Quantum Computers Struggle With Errors

Quantum computers rely on qubits to store and process information in ways that conventional computers cannot. Many current systems use superconducting circuits, but these are highly sensitive to electromagnetic noise and stray electrical charges - similar to the static electricity that can make hair cling to a balloon.

Even very small disturbances can disrupt the fragile quantum information stored in a qubit. As more qubits are added, controlling those errors becomes increasingly difficult, making scalability one of the central challenges in quantum computing.

In a study published in npj Quantum Information, researchers from Surrey's Quantum Sciences Group describe a different approach built around superfluid helium-3 - an unusual form of liquid helium that can flow without friction.

Their proposed device, called the Superfluid Helium Oscillator Quantum (SHOQ) device, would use charge-neutral superfluid helium. Because the helium carries no electric charge, the design could be naturally protected from certain types of electromagnetic noise.

A Qubit With Much Lower Predicted Error Rates

According to the researchers, this is the first reported design for a qubit based on a superfluid. Their calculations suggest the SHOQ device could have error rates about 100 times lower than conventional superconducting qubits.

Dr. Priya Sharma, Daphne Jackson Fellow in Hybrid Quantum Systems at the University of Surrey's School of Mathematics and Physics, and lead author of the study, said:

"We are not the first to think about the individual components behind this idea, but what we have done for the first time is bring them together in a microfluidic device and work out the specific details that could enable the device to function as a qubit.

"The maths tells us that it should work. We have taken what we already know about superfluid helium and quantum technologies and turned that into an educated design, with the parameters and specifications needed to build one. The next step is to make a prototype and put those predictions to the test."

Rather than replacing existing quantum computing hardware, the researchers say the SHOQ device could potentially be integrated with superconducting quantum technology.

That could allow different types of qubits to perform different jobs within the same system, depending on their strengths.

One longer-term possibility is to use the SHOQ device as a form of quantum memory. In that setup, a superfluid based qubit could store quantum information while other hardware carries out calculations.

Dr. Eran Ginossar, Associate Professor at the University of Surrey's Department of Physics and Advanced Technology Institute, and co-author of the study, said:

"We don't necessarily need one type of qubit to do everything. Combining different quantum technologies could allow us to take advantage of the strengths of each.

"Superfluid helium gives us a fundamentally different type of quantum hardware to explore. If the predicted performance can be demonstrated experimentally, it could eventually work alongside existing superconducting technologies as part of a larger quantum system."

The team now plans to build a prototype to find out whether the predicted performance can be reproduced in a real device. That effort is being supported by an IAA Commercialisation Fellowship awarded to Dr. Priya Sharma.

The SHOQ device would need to operate at extremely low temperatures, but researchers have already reached the necessary conditions in previous experiments involving superfluid helium-3.

The project was led by the University of Surrey in collaboration with Professor Jens Koch at Northwestern University in the United States. Koch was one of the researchers involved in developing the transmon - a superconducting qubit design that is now widely used in quantum computing.

Materials provided by University of Surrey. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10475. Your SSL certificates can now last 200 days at most: The real risk isn’t renewal

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every SSL certificate comes with a limited lifespan. Following the latest update from the CA/Browser Forum, the maximum lifetime of a certificate has fallen from 398 days to 200, and it will drop to 100 days in March 2027 and 47 days in 2029. Renewal is no longer an annual chore. It’s an ongoing task that only stays manageable with automation.

So what does this mean if you already look after sites with SSL certificates, and will it bring drastic changes? For many setups, very little will change. For others, one missed renewal can mean an outage. This article explains what’s changing and what to do if you’re in the second group.

Today, an SSL certificate is valid for 200 days at most. The limit applies when a certificate is issued, so the ones you already hold keep their original expiration date.

Where you feel it depends on how your certificates renew. Sites on managed hosting platforms or behind a content delivery network (CDN) usually renew automatically already, often every 90 days, so the new limit changes little. The pressure lands on setups where someone buys a certificate once a year and installs it by hand. At 47 days, that becomes about 12 renewals a year.

A certificate is a promise from a certificate authority that a key really belongs to a domain, and that promise gets less reliable the longer it runs. If a private key leaks, or a certificate is issued by mistake, it stays usable until it expires. Cutting the lifetime shrinks that window, so a problem that once lingered for over a year is now fixed in weeks.

Shorter lifetimes also make revocation, the process for canceling a bad certificate, far less critical. Revocation has never worked perfectly, but a certificate that expires within weeks matters much less if a cancellation doesn’t reach every browser.

Another reason is about people. A task done once a year is easy to forget and hard to check from outside. Browser makers have pushed for years to move the industry onto automatic renewal, on the logic that a process that runs every few weeks gets tested constantly. If it breaks, you notice quickly instead of discovering it on the day the certificate expires.

For most people, the first effect is simply more work. A certificate that once needed attention every year will soon need it every few weeks. Plus, each renewal is a small chance for something to go wrong. Multiply that by dozens of sites, mail servers, and devices, and the chance of one slipping through grows quickly.

The safety nets are also thinner. If you relied on the renewal emails, nothing is watching your certificates now. Additionally, with a shorter life, there’s less time between this should have renewed and this has expired.

An expired certificate doesn’t make a site slow or dated. It makes it unreachable for most visitors. Chrome, Firefox, and Safari all show a full-page warning that the connection isn’t private, and most people leave instead of clicking through. If the site uses HSTS, a setting that tells browsers to connect only over secure links, there is often no way to click through at all, and visitors are blocked outright until the certificate is fixed.

The damage doesn’t stop at the home page. Checkouts, contact forms, and logins that depend on the secure connection fail along with it, often without an error message that points at the real cause. Other systems break, too, because apps and integrations that call your site over a secure link refuse to connect to an expired certificate.

Nothing about your site has to change for any of this to happen. A date passes, and a site that worked on Friday is down on Monday. In a 47-day lifetime, that date comes around about every six weeks.

Automatic renewal tools do the heavy lifting well. The failures happen around them:

Look at the five failures above and you’ll see what they share. Each one happens quietly. The renewal job ran, or looked like it did, and nobody checked what the server was actually serving afterwards. Automation can tell you it tried. It can’t tell you the result reached your visitors.

What closes that gap is a check that never relies on your server’s own account of events. Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring does exactly that. It reads the certificate installed on your web server from the cloud, the way a visitor’s browser would, and alerts you before it expires, using thresholds you choose. If a renewal fails quietly, you hear about it from the monitor and not from a customer.

For each certificate, it records the issuing authority, the owner, the issue date, the expiration date, and the days remaining, including on servers that host several certificates behind one address. That last number matters more as lifespans shrink. When a certificate lasts 47 days, the days left is the figure that tells you a renewal is overdue, and you can set your warning against it using the quarter-of-the-lifetime rule covered next.

Once a monitor is watching your certificates, the next question is when it should speak up.

Most expiration alerts are set to a fixed number of days: warn at 30 days left and escalate at seven. That works for a certificate that lasts a year and renews with a month to spare. It breaks when the whole certificate lasts for a limited number of days.

Here’s why: a healthy certificate renews with about a third of its life still left. If your alert appears earlier than that, it goes off on certificates that are doing nothing wrong, and soon everyone ignores it. As lifespans shrink, a fixed 30-day alert will start doing exactly that.

The fix is to set the alerts based on lateness, not on how close the expiry date is. Warn when a quarter of the lifetime is left, since a renewal is overdue by then, and escalate when a tenth is left. Because these are shares of the lifetime, they keep working, however short lifespans get.

The next cut lands on March 15, 2027, so a few checks now will save you from scrambling later:

Shorter lifespans won’t break your site, but a missed renewal will, so fix the quiet failures now while a mistake still costs an afternoon to fix, not a Monday morning outage affecting lots of customers.

If you’d like a second pair of eyes on your certificates, you can start a 30-day free trial of Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring.

#10471. Scientists recreate a bizarre quantum effect that stops a particle cold

A particle does not have to pass through a magnetic field to be influenced by it. That surprising feature of quantum physics has now been recreated by researchers at the University of Oxford, who used a hybrid quantum computer to demonstrate how an invisible magnetic influence can bring a simulated particle's movement to a complete stop.

The experiment reproduced a phenomenon known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect, in which a charged particle traveling around a region containing magnetic flux experiences a measurable change in its quantum state, even though its path never takes it through the magnetic field itself.

The Oxford team achieved this using a quantum system that combines qubits, the basic units of quantum information, with quantum oscillators, which represent vibrating motion. Their findings, published in Nature Physics, demonstrate how this combination could help scientists investigate fundamental interactions between matter and fields that are increasingly difficult to simulate using conventional computers.

A Strange Quantum Effect Predicted in 1959

In everyday physics, a charged particle responds to the electric and magnetic fields it encounters. Quantum mechanics, however, reveals that particles can behave in ways that have no straightforward counterpart in our ordinary experience.

In 1959, physicists Yakir Aharonov and David Bohm predicted that a charged particle could be affected by magnetic flux even if it traveled entirely outside the region containing the magnetic field. Rather than experiencing a conventional magnetic force along its path, the particle would undergo a change in its quantum phase.

Quantum phase describes an aspect of a particle's wave behavior that influences how different possible paths interfere with one another. Although the phase itself is not directly visible, differences in phase can produce measurable effects.

The prediction became known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect and was eventually verified in experiments involving real electrons. It remains one of the clearest demonstrations that the quantum world operates according to rules that go beyond the familiar laws of classical physics.

Researchers are now exploring how this phenomenon behaves in more complex theoretical systems known as lattice gauge theories.

These theories describe how matter interacts with fields associated with fundamental forces. They represent space as a grid, with matter located at individual points and fields associated with the connections between them. Such models are particularly important in particle physics and high-energy physics, where scientists study the basic particles and interactions that make up the universe.

The challenge is that the mathematics becomes extraordinarily demanding as these systems grow more complicated. Even powerful conventional computers can struggle to calculate how all the interacting components evolve.

Quantum computers offer a different approach. Instead of attempting to calculate every aspect of a quantum system using classical computing methods, scientists can construct another quantum system that follows the same underlying rules. They can then observe its behavior directly, an approach known as quantum simulation.

How Scientists Built the Quantum Experiment

The Oxford project began in 2022, when lead author Dr. Sebastian Saner, Dr. Oana Bazavan, and their colleagues started developing a way to simulate lattice gauge theories using a hybrid quantum system. They collaborated with Dr. Alejandro Bermudez of the Instituto de Física Teórica in Madrid.

Their experimental design relied on two different quantum components that performed separate functions.

The first consisted of qubits encoded in the internal states of trapped ions, which are electrically charged atoms held in place using electromagnetic fields. These qubits represented the gauge fields in the theoretical model.

The second component used quantum oscillators, created by the vibrations of those same trapped ions. These oscillators represented matter.

By combining the two, the researchers constructed a simple loop that served as a basic building block of a lattice gauge theory. Two oscillators represented matter at two different locations, while two qubits represented the fields connecting those locations.

Although the arrangement was small, it was sufficient to reproduce an important interaction between matter and magnetic flux.

Quantum Entanglement Creates a Surprising Solution

The researchers next placed the two qubits into an entangled quantum state. Entanglement is a phenomenon in which quantum systems become linked in ways that cannot be fully explained by classical physics.

Within the team's simulated lattice gauge theory, this entangled state represented magnetic flux passing through the center of the loop.

Initially, the researchers had planned to introduce the magnetic flux as a fixed background condition. However, creating the necessary interaction would have required capabilities that their quantum hardware did not possess.

Encoding the flux directly into the qubits provided a practical alternative. More importantly, it allowed the magnetic flux to become part of the evolving quantum system rather than remaining an unchanging external influence.

"For us, the exciting step was to encode the magnetic flux in a gauge field that was itself dynamical," says Dr. Saner. "Rather than having matter evolve in a fixed background, the matter and gauge field become part of the same quantum dynamics."

What began as a technical workaround ultimately enabled the researchers to explore a more interesting version of the phenomenon, one in which matter and the field could participate together in the quantum dynamics.

A Quantum Effect That Stops Particle Motion

With their simulated loop in place, the scientists examined how a matter particle moved between locations through a process known as quantum tunneling.

Quantum tunneling allows particles to move between states in ways that would not be possible under ordinary classical rules.

When the researchers ran their simulation without magnetic flux, the particle could tunnel freely around the loop.

Introducing the simulated flux produced a strikingly different result.

The particle had two possible paths around the loop, but the magnetic flux changed their relative quantum phases. As a result, the two paths interfered destructively, meaning their quantum contributions canceled each other out.

This interference completely suppressed the tunneling, leaving the simulated particle frozen in its original state.

The outcome demonstrated the Aharonov-Bohm effect within a lattice gauge theory containing a dynamical field. Rather than simply reproducing the familiar behavior of a particle moving around a fixed magnetic influence, the experiment showed how the phenomenon can emerge when matter and the field are both represented by interacting quantum components.

A New Tool for Exploring Fundamental Physics

The Oxford findings were published alongside a related study by researchers at the University of Maryland, led by Professor Norbert Linke.

The Maryland team also used a hybrid quantum system, but their experiment focused on simulating the Yukawa potential, a mathematical description of an interaction relevant to nuclear and particle physics.

Although the two research groups developed their methods independently, they later coordinated the submission of their studies to Nature Physics.

Together, the experiments demonstrate how hybrid quantum computers could become valuable tools for investigating fundamental interactions that are difficult to reproduce using classical simulations.

By combining different types of quantum components, researchers can build systems in which matter and fields are represented in ways that more closely reflect the physics they want to study.

The Oxford experiment provides an example of that potential. A phenomenon first predicted in 1959 and later observed with electrons has now been recreated in a quantum simulator where the field itself participates in the dynamics, offering scientists another way to explore the unusual rules governing the quantum world.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10464. Logging AI Prompts and Responses in Java Without Leaking Sensitive Data

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picture this. A customer writes to your support chatbot: "Hi, I'm Asha, my card ending 4111 was charged twice, call me on 98400 12345." Your Java service wraps that message in a prompt, sends it to a model, and logs the whole thing so you can debug it later. Congratulations, you now have a customer's name, phone number and card details sitting in a plain text log file that half the engineering team can open.

This happens more often than people admit. Logging AI calls is genuinely useful, but prompts and responses are some of the most personal text your application ever touches. Here's how to keep the usefulness and drop the risk.

A regular web request is easy to log: URL, status code, time taken. AI calls are messier.

The output changes from run to run. If a user says "your bot gave me a wrong answer yesterday", the only proof of what happened is what you sent and what came back at that moment. You can't just replay it and expect the same result.

Prompts are also built, not typed. The final text is usually a template, a few retrieved document chunks, some chat history and the user's message stitched together. When something looks off, the bug could be in any of those pieces, and only the assembled prompt shows you which one.

Then there's cost. You pay by the token, so without per-call records you can't say which feature is burning money. And quality drifts over time as models get upgraded and templates get edited. Old logs are your only baseline.

So yes, you need these logs. The real question is what goes into them.

Before writing any masking code, agree on what counts as sensitive. In most business apps that means:

Names, emails, phone numbers and home addresses

Card numbers, bank details and government ID numbers

Health, legal or job-related details people mention in free text

Internal material like contract wording, pricing rules or source code

The first three groups follow patterns, so they're easy to catch. The last two don't, and that's where most teams get caught out. Keep that in mind, because it shapes the design below.

The safest sensitive value is the one that never reaches the log. So split what you record into two buckets.

The first is metadata: a request ID, a hashed user or tenant ID, model name, prompt template name and version, token counts, latency, retry count, error type and estimated cost. None of this is personal, and it answers most day-to-day questions.

The second is content, meaning the actual prompt and response text. You only need this when you're investigating quality, and even then you rarely need all of it. A sensible default is to log metadata for every call and masked content for a small sample, maybe five percent, plus every failed call.

Here's a mistake that's easy to make: letting the log appender clean things up afterwards. By then the raw text may already be in a buffer, an async queue or a crash dump. Mask first, then log.

The cleanest way is one small component that every AI log call has to pass through:

public interface PiiMasker {     String mask(String input); }

Nothing else is allowed to write raw prompts. One chokepoint is much easier to review and test than masking sprinkled across fifty classes.

For structured data like emails, cards and tokens, regular expressions do the job well:

public class RegexPiiMasker implements PiiMasker {     private static final Map RULES = new LinkedHashMap<>();     static {         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("[A-Za-z0-9.%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}"), "[EMAIL]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\d[ -]?){13,19}\b"), "[CARD]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\+?\d{1,3}[ -]?)?\d{10}\b"), "[PHONE]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("(?i)bearer\s+[A-Za-z0-9.~+/=-]+"), "Bearer [TOKEN]");     }     @Override     public String mask(String input) {         if (input == null || input.isBlank()) return input;         String result = input;         for (var rule : RULES.entrySet()) {             result = rule.getKey().matcher(result).replaceAll(rule.getValue());         }         return result;     } }

A few small choices matter here. The patterns are compiled once, not on every call. The order is fixed, because a card number can otherwise get half eaten by the phone rule. And the replacement labels such as [EMAIL] keep the sentence readable, so whoever debugs it later can still follow what the user was asking.

Regex won't catch a name like "Asha Raman" or an address buried mid-sentence. For those you have a few options, and they work best together.

You can run a named-entity recognition model that tags people, places and organisations, then mask anything above a confidence threshold. You can do exact replacement of values you already know, such as the customer's name from the current session. Or, best of all, you can mask at the field level while building the prompt. If your code knows that customer.getEmail() is going into the template, swap in a placeholder right there instead of trying to spot it afterwards. Knowing beats guessing every time.

Plain redaction can remove too much. If the same email shows up five times in a conversation, five identical [EMAIL] tags hide the fact that it was one person.

The fix is consistent pseudonyms. Turn each real value into a short token using a keyed hash, so the same email always becomes something like EMAIL_7f3a. You can still follow the thread, but nobody can read the original from the log. Use an HMAC with a secret from your secrets manager. A plain unkeyed hash of a phone number can be brute-forced in minutes, because there just aren't that many phone numbers.

One warning: if you also store a lookup table to reverse the tokens, that table is now sensitive data too. Most teams are better off with one-way tokens.

Free-text log lines are hard to search and easy to leak through. Write one JSON record per AI call with clearly separate fields:

log.info("ai_call",     kv("requestId", requestId),     kv("model", model),     kv("promptVersion", "support-reply-v4"),     kv("inputTokens", usage.inputTokens()),     kv("outputTokens", usage.outputTokens()),     kv("latencyMs", elapsed),     kv("promptMasked", masker.mask(prompt)),     kv("responseMasked", masker.mask(response)));

Now dashboards for cost and latency never need to touch the text fields at all. You can also keep the two kinds of data for different lengths of time: metadata for a year, masked content for a couple of weeks. Put the request ID into your logging framework's MDC so every line belonging to one call, including retries and tool calls, carries the same ID.

Streaming trips people up. Tokens arrive in tiny pieces, and a card number can be split across two chunks, which means masking each chunk on its own will miss it. Collect the full response in a buffer, mask it once the stream ends, and log that. If the stream dies halfway, run the partial buffer through the same masker.

Tool calls are the other trap. When the model calls one of your Java methods, the arguments and return values are often the most sensitive thing in the whole exchange: order details, account balances, addresses. Treat them as part of the prompt and response. Log the tool name, duration and outcome by default, and log arguments only after masking.

Masking lowers the risk but never removes it, so surround it with some ordinary discipline:

Give content fields a short retention period. Old prompt archives are liabilities.

Limit who can read AI logs. Debugging a model doesn't mean someone needs to read customer messages.

Encrypt log storage and the pipeline that feeds it.

Log the hashed user ID so you can find and delete one person's records when they ask.

Allow verbose logging in development with fake data, and enforce strict settings in production through configuration, not through people remembering.

A masker that quietly stops working is worse than having none, because everyone assumes the logs are clean. Write tests for it:

@Test void masksEmailAndCardNumber() {     String raw = "Contact me at asha@example.com, card 4111 1111 1111 1111";     String safe = new RegexPiiMasker().mask(raw);     assertFalse(safe.contains("asha@example.com"));     assertFalse(safe.contains("4111"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[EMAIL]"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[CARD]")); }

Build a small set of messy, realistic samples: numbers with odd spacing, mixed languages, addresses inside sentences, text pasted from spreadsheets. Run them in your CI pipeline, and add a new case every time a leak turns up. A scheduled job that scans stored logs for patterns that should never appear makes a good safety net too.

A few I'd watch for. Logging raw text at debug level and assuming production never enables it; debug flags get switched on during incidents, so mask at every level. Masking only the user's message while forgetting retrieved documents, system prompts and tool results. Letting SDK exceptions carry the full request body into the logs. Leaving wire-level HTTP logging on in production, which bypasses your masker completely. And treating masking as finished once it ships, when every new prompt template can bring new kinds of data.

Every AI call writes a structured metadata record with a request ID

All prompt and response text goes through a single masker first

Streams are masked after assembly, and tool arguments are masked too

Content logging is sampled, short-lived and access-controlled

Tests cover the masker, with a regression case for every past leak

Client libraries aren't dumping raw payloads into the logs

Good logs turn an AI feature from a black box into something you can actually debug, price and improve. The same logs can also expose the people who trusted you with their details. The way through is fairly plain: record metadata for every call, push all content through one well-tested masker, use pseudonyms when you need to follow a conversation, handle streams and tools carefully, and keep retention short. Set that up before launch, not after the first incident, and you get visibility without regret.

#10462. New OpenAI and Anthropic models sound alarms on safety and control, report

Frontier AI models that have yet to be released by OpenAI and Anthropic are reportedly reaching the point where employees believe they cannot be safely released or controlled, employees reportedly say.

Axios cites “more than two dozen” employees inside of OpenAI and Anthropic who are sounding warnings around unreleased models from both companies.

While both OpenAI’s GPT and Anthropic’s Claude public models are increasingly powerful, these employees say that new models behind the scenes are starting to beat out the technical solutions for controlling them. This has led to debates between those who think they’ll be able to fix these problems, and others who believe companies should stop training new models altogether due to the risks.

If we continue trying to make models much smarter, such that they’re actually smarter than us … they will in fact be able to trick us successfully. And once you get to that point, many of the schemes that we’ve had at work up until now sort of go out the window.

The report goes out to point out that some researchers feel that executives at major AI companies have become “out of touch” with the severity of safety risks, though Axios was told that OpenAI is adopting new safety practices while Anthropic said that “Keeping a model internal doesn’t make it safe,” adding that “we may slow down or hold back even when it costs us.”

#10461. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

Materials provided by University of East Anglia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

There is an embarrassment of riches if you're looking to upgrade to a Wi-Fi 7 router for Black Friday

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Mesh routers are among the hottest-selling types of routers in networking, and leading the charge are products based on the Wi-Fi 7 standard. Mesh routers enable you to fully cover your home or office with wireless coverage that would otherwise be impossible with a single, standalone router.

Fortunately, numerous manufacturers are producing Wi-Fi 7 mesh routers, offering a wide range of options to choose from. If you prefer seamless integration with your Amazon account and Alexa services, consider opting for the Eero Pro 7. If you want endless configuration options and no-cost features, such as extensive Parental Controls, the Asus ZenWiFi BT4 has you covered. If you're looking for a delicate balance of performance and features at a bargain price, consider the TP-Link Deco BE95.

However, one thing to keep in mind when shopping for a network upgrade is that not all Wi-Fi 7 routers are created equal. While there are speed differences between products that depend on several factors (channel width, total number of streams, etc.), the primary differentiator among Wi-Fi 7 routers comes down to the number of supported bands. There are dual- and tri-band routers, with the latter offering the best potential performance. Dual-band routers only support the 2.4 GHz and 5 GHz bands, while tri-band routers support the higher-performing and shorter-range 6 GHz band. The maximum performance of the 6 GHz band is what gives tri-band Wi-Fi 7 routers a significant theoretical speed advantage over their Wi-Fi 6E predecessors.

Some manufacturers, such as Asus, also offer quad-band routers that feature either two 5 GHz wireless bands or two 6 GHz bands.

With that said, check out the extensive list below for all the best deals on Wi-Fi 7 routers from Asus, Eero, Netgear, MSI, and TP-Link.

The Asus ZenWiFi BD4 is an entry-level Wi-Fi 7 router and thus only supports two bands (2.4 GHz and 5 GHz). This 3-pack features up to 3.6 Gbps of combined throughput and covers 6,500 square feet.

The Deco BE23 is TP-Link's most entry-level Wi-Fi 7 router, sporting dual-band wireless, up to 3.6 Gbps throughput, and coverage of 4,500 square feet.

The Deco BE63 is a mid-range, tri-band Wi-Fi 7 router with four 2.5 GbE ports, 10 Gbps of combined throughput, and coverage of 5,800 square feet.

The TP-Link Deco 7 Pro BE10000 is a tri-band, Wi-Fi 7 mesh system that includes three wireless nodes. Each node comes with four 2.5 GbE ports, and the total wireless coverage is 7,600 square feet.

TP-Link's flagship Deco BE95 Wi-Fi 7 mesh router supports four bands, delivering up to 33 Gbps of throughput. It also features two 10 GbE ports per node.

The Eero Pro 7 is a mid-range, tri-band Wi-Fi 7 router that offers coverage of up to 4,000 square feet. Each wireless node features two 5 GbE ports.

The Eero Max 7 is Amazon's flagship Wi-Fi 7 router, featuring tri-band wireless, two 2.5 GbE ports, two 10 GbE ports, and up to 5,000 square feet of coverage.

The Orbi 770 is the latest in Netgear's long line of Orbi mesh routers. This one is a tri-band system with coverage of up to 5,500 square feet and 11 Gbps of combined throughput. You also get three 2.5 GbE ports and one 2.5 GbE port for WAN.

MSI's Roamii BE Lite is a newcomer on the Wi-Fi 7 mesh router scene, and it's a dual-band unit. Each node includes 2.5 GbE ports, and MSI even provides wall mounts in the box.

The MSI Roamii BE Pro is a high-powered, tri-band Wi-Fi 7 mesh router that won't break the bank. Each node includes four 2.5 GbE ports for connecting your wired devices, and the mesh system is controlled by a rather feature-packed smartphone app.

If you want a high-performance mesh router, look no further. The Asus ZenWiFi BE30000 is a quad-band solution, featuring three wireless nodes. Aggregate speeds across all four bands (you get two 6 GHz bands, one of which is dedicated to a wireless backhaul) are 30 Gbps, and you get coverage for up to 11,000 square feet.

#10455. How to Build a Unified Lead Tracking Pipeline for Web Forms and Phone Calls

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor clicks a search ad, submits a quote request, and calls your sales team ten minutes later. The form integration creates a CRM contact. The phone platform records a call. By Friday, the marketing report shows two leads, while sales remembers one conversation that turned into a deal.

Both systems captured something useful. The problem is that neither record explains how the two interactions belong together.

A unified tracking pipeline gives forms and calls a common event format, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to CRM outcomes. It also needs to survive a less tidy reality: repeated callbacks, missing campaign data, delayed updates, and a CRM that occasionally refuses requests.

This tutorial builds the core of that pipeline with JavaScript and PostgreSQL. We will capture campaign parameters, define a shared event contract, and persist events alongside the work needed to deliver them. Provider authentication and CRM requests belong in adapters around this core, using the documentation for the services you choose.

Before writing an endpoint, decide what you are counting. A phone call can produce several status callbacks. A person can submit two forms. An existing customer can contact sales about a new purchase.

Use separate identifiers for these different things:

This separation prevents two common mistakes. Deduplicating by call ID alone can discard later status updates. Counting every callback as a lead inflates demand.

For this implementation, each event is unique within a tenant and source. A tenant is the business whose data you are processing; the source identifies a website or provider account. The same provider event ID can therefore appear in two accounts without causing a collision.

Keep the original interactions even after you connect them to one contact. The form and the call are both useful evidence. Your reporting layer decides whether they represent one opportunity.

Campaign parameters are easiest to capture when the visitor first arrives. Waiting until the form submission can lose them if the visitor has already moved to another page.

Google's campaign URL documentation explains the roles of utm_source, utm_medium, and utm_campaign. The following helper reads those fields and a few optional ones, without copying the entire query string:

const params = new URL(url).searchParams;

"utm_source", "utm_medium", "utm_campaign",

return Object.fromEntries(keys.flatMap(key => {

The browser's URLSearchParams interface handles query parsing and decoding. It saves you from writing string-splitting code that breaks on encoded characters.

Run this helper through your site's existing consent and attribution flow. Where storage is permitted, associate the result with an opaque attribution reference in your backend and retain that reference through the visit. A later form submission sends the reference; the server resolves it within the correct tenant and visitor session. The form must still work when attribution storage is unavailable.

Keep first-touch and current-touch records separately if you need both. Overwriting the first campaign every time someone returns makes the eventual report answer a different question.

Treat campaign labels as untrusted input. Visitors can edit query strings and hidden fields. Validate them on the server, escape them when displayed, and never use them as authorization or as the sole evidence for commission payments. Avoid putting names, email addresses, or other personal information in UTM values.

Missing tags also need careful handling. An empty UTM object only tells you that no accepted UTM values were captured. Classifying a visit as direct, organic, or referral requires additional evidence and an explicit attribution rule.

Forms carry browser context naturally. Phone calls need a bridge between the number dialed and the marketing interaction that prompted the call.

There are two useful levels of precision. A number assigned to one campaign can identify that campaign. Dynamic number insertion can support a more detailed association by displaying tracking numbers according to a visitor or session mapping. That association depends on the provider's number allocation and expiry rules.

Providers differ in how they implement this, and in what they expose beyond it, such as keyword-level reporting or conversation analysis. If you are still choosing one, this comparison of attribution software with call tracking is a useful starting point. At the integration boundary, check which event identifiers, attribution fields, delivery mechanisms, and authentication options your configured product exposes.

Keep that provider-specific work in an adapter. Its job is to turn an authenticated notification into your event contract and resolve any attribution reference supported by the provider's data. The application schema below is an internal contract; each provider adapter translates its documented payload into that contract.

Store the basis of a match with the attribution record. For example, session_match, campaign_number, and unknown make different levels of evidence visible to reporting. If a number has been reassigned, use the mapping valid when the call occurred, not whichever mapping happens to be current when the callback arrives. If the evidence is ambiguous, leave the association unresolved.

Caller ID is a separate issue. A shared office number, a withheld number, or an outdated contact record can make automatic identity matching unreliable. A confirmed CRM contact ID or an authenticated account provides a stronger link. Treat a matching phone number as a candidate for reconciliation rather than proof that two records belong to the same person.

A shared event envelope lets the rest of your application work with a small, predictable vocabulary. Here is an illustrative call event:

"occurred_at": "2026-11-06T10:14:00.000Z",

The event ID identifies this occurrence. The interaction ID groups it with other events for the same call. contact_ref can remain empty until you have enough evidence to connect the interaction to a contact.

Keep contact details in a controlled contact store and campaign details in an attribution store. The references above point to those records; they are not credentials and must not grant access on their own.

The following factory checks the shared envelope. Each adapter still needs its own validation for provider fields and the contents of data.

"form.submitted", "call.started", "call.completed",

export function makeEvent(input, trusted) {

const ids = [trusted.tenantId, trusted.source,

if (ids.some(id => typeof id !== "string" ||

throw new TypeError("Invalid event identifiers");

throw new TypeError("Unsupported event type");

const time = Date.parse(input.occurredAt);

if (typeof input.occurredAt !== "string" ||

new Date(time).toISOString() !== input.occurredAt) {

throw new TypeError("Expected a canonical UTC timestamp");

attribution_ref: input.attributionRef ?? null,

Resolve trusted from server configuration and the authenticated integration. Never let a submitted tenant_id choose whose records a webhook can write. Resolve contact and attribution references within that same tenant.

For a form, issue a submission ID and reuse it when retrying the same submission. Generate a new one when the visitor deliberately starts another inquiry. Preserve the originally accepted timestamp as well. For a phone event, prefer the provider's stable event ID. If the provider documents a sequence number, a call ID plus that sequence can identify an occurrence within a provider account.

Twilio's call resource documentation provides a concrete example: call progress callbacks include a SequenceNumber, and separate HTTP requests can arrive out of order. Preserve that sequence in the adapter. A late “ringing” event should remain in the history without replacing a newer terminal state.

Authenticate before calling this factory. Use the provider's supported validator and the exact request representation it requires. Twilio's webhook security guide describes signature validation, including the importance of the request URL and payload. Other providers may use different mechanisms. Public form endpoints need their own input limits, abuse controls, and appropriate CSRF protection.

Consider a failure between these two operations: the application saves an event, then crashes before scheduling its CRM update. The event exists, but nothing is waiting to deliver it.

A transactional outbox addresses that gap. Save the event and a delivery task in the same database transaction, then let a worker process the task. AWS explains this dual-write problem in its transactional outbox guidance.

Here is a compact schema. received_at records ingestion time, while the envelope retains the event's original occurred_at value.

received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,

available_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

REFERENCES lead_events (tenant_id, source, event_id)

The ingestion function below accepts a pg pool and a normalized event. Install pg in your Node.js project, save the function as persist.mjs, and initialize the pool using your application's database configuration.

export async function persistEvent(pool, event) {

const key = [event.tenant_id, event.source, event.event_id];

const values = [...key, JSON.stringify(event)];

await client.query("BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED");

ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, source, event_id) DO NOTHING

WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND source = $2 AND event_id = $3

const error = new Error("Event ID reused with new data");

INSERT INTO crm_outbox (tenant_id, source, event_id)

return { inserted: inserted.rowCount === 1 };

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation defines the conflict handling used here. The unique key makes duplicate detection hold across application processes. A duplicate with the same normalized payload creates no second task; reuse of the key with different data raises a conflict for investigation.

This depends on immutable event bodies. Do not add a fresh receipt timestamp, random identifier, or current CRM status to the normalized payload on each delivery. Store later enrichment separately or emit a new event. Otherwise an ordinary retry will appear to be a conflicting event.

The function deliberately uses one database client throughout the transaction, as required by the node-postgres transaction documentation. Parameter values are passed separately from SQL, following its parameterized query guidance.

After this function commits, acknowledge the notification using the response expected by the sender. An identical duplicate should also receive a successful acknowledgement. If persistence fails, return the appropriate failure response and rely on the configured retry or reconciliation process. A success response sent before durable storage can turn a temporary database failure into a missing event.

For voice integrations, distinguish an informational status callback from a request asking your application how to handle a live call. They can require different response bodies, as explained in the Twilio webhooks FAQ.

The outbox gives you durable work to process. It does not make a remote CRM operation happen exactly once.

Imagine the CRM saves an update, but the worker crashes before setting processed_at. When the task runs again, the same request may reach the CRM twice. Design for that case explicitly.

Use a stable external identifier for each contact, opportunity, and activity, and check which deduplication guarantees the destination supports. HubSpot's contacts API documentation, for example, describes upserts using unique properties. Support for a contact upsert does not automatically make every associated activity or workflow idempotent. Verify the behavior of each operation you send.

If the destination cannot deduplicate a create request and a timeout leaves its result uncertain, reconcile that operation before repeating it. A local “sent” flag alone cannot prove whether the remote write happened.

The worker should claim due tasks, commit a recoverable lease, and perform network calls outside the database transaction. A lease allows another worker to recover work after a crash. When multiple workers poll the outbox, PostgreSQL's SKIP LOCKED behavior can help claim separate rows, but the lease and its recovery logic still need to be implemented.

Retry transient failures with increasing delays and jitter. Respect the destination's rate-limit instructions. Send persistent validation failures to a review queue with a useful error category, and provide a way to replay them after correction. The schema above is the ingestion foundation; a production worker needs lease fields, failure details, and a retry policy appropriate to its destination.

Also preserve ordering where it changes meaning. If two events update the same contact or opportunity, process them serially or reject stale updates using a stored version. A historical event should not move a won opportunity back to an earlier stage.

Once both channels reach the CRM, connect them to the result the business cares about. A form submission is an inquiry. A completed phone call is a technical outcome. Neither alone establishes that a lead qualified or a purchase happened. If agents record call dispositions, such as whether a call was qualified or transferred to a closer, that field is a richer signal than call completion status alone.

Have the CRM or the system that owns the transaction emit explicit outcome events, such as lead.qualified and deal.won. Include the internal opportunity ID and the original outcome time. Model reversals or cancellations explicitly if they affect revenue reporting.

Then choose the unit behind each metric. For a report about opportunities acquired during a particular month, a qualification rate can use qualified opportunities divided by all opportunities in that acquisition cohort. Counting qualification events received during the month would answer a different question and could include older inquiries.

Deduplicate opportunities before calculating channel totals. A person who submits a form and calls about the same purchase should retain both interactions, while the opportunity receives credit according to your chosen attribution rule. Label that rule in the report so first-touch and last-touch numbers are not compared as though they measure the same thing.

Measure data quality alongside performance. Track unresolved attribution, duplicate deliveries, ingestion failures, and the age of the oldest pending outbox task. Once several sources and destinations feed the same reports, a data observability platform can watch these signals across the whole pipeline instead of relying on checks inside a single worker. An apparently quiet campaign may actually have a broken integration.

Operational logs should contain enough context to investigate failures without becoming another customer database. Use internal references and error categories, and avoid dumping webhook bodies into logs. The OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet provides guidance on excluding or protecting sensitive values.

A successful form submission proves little about retries or missing data. Before connecting production traffic, test the cases that could distort reporting or lose an inquiry.

Run database cases against PostgreSQL with separate connections so the tests exercise actual constraints and transaction behavior. Use the provider's test tools or signed fixtures for webhook authentication. Test CRM retries against a sandbox with the destination's real duplicate-handling rules.

Start rollout with one form and one phone source. Trace a known inquiry from its captured campaign through its interactions to its CRM outcome. Reconcile event totals against the source systems over the same time window, allowing for delivery delays. Expand once that path is explainable and failures are visible.

The same adapter pattern extends to inquiries that start offline, such as leads collected at a trade show and exported as a CSV or XLSX file. Import each row as a form.submitted event with the event as its source, a stable row identifier as event_id, and the original capture time as occurred_at. Because the outbox treats an identical event as a duplicate, re-importing the same export won’t create a second CRM task.

What is a unified lead tracking pipeline?

It is a workflow that converts inquiries from different channels into a shared data model, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to business outcomes. Forms and calls keep their own interaction records, while contacts and opportunities provide the relationships needed for reporting.

Can I connect a phone call to a website visit?

Sometimes. Dynamic number insertion can provide a session or visitor association when the provider's mapping is available and valid. A campaign-specific number usually supports campaign-level attribution. If the caller uses a saved number, switches devices, or calls after a mapping expires, the available evidence may be less precise.

Handle duplicate delivery and identity resolution separately. Stable event IDs and a database constraint prevent repeated notifications from producing repeated work. Connecting several interactions to one contact or opportunity requires a separate matching policy, with ambiguous cases left unresolved until there is enough evidence.

Should a completed call count as a conversion?

Only if that is the conversion your report explicitly measures. A completed call does not establish that a prospect qualified or bought something. Keep technical call status separate from CRM outcomes and use the latter when reporting qualified demand or revenue.

Do I need a message broker for this architecture?

You can begin with PostgreSQL and an outbox worker if that setup meets your throughput and latency needs. A broker becomes useful when several consumers need independent delivery, or when buffering and scale justify the additional operations. In either case, retries still require idempotent consumers.

What happens when tracking consent is unavailable?

Keep the inquiry flow usable and apply your site's existing consent rules to optional attribution collection and storage. Accept permitted form or call data, leave unavailable attribution empty, and avoid reconstructing a visitor identity through fingerprinting. Reports should expose that missing context instead of inventing a campaign match.

#10453. Samsung officially starts rolling out Wear OS 7 to these older Galaxy Watch models

Following its debut on the Galaxy Watch 9 earlier this year, Samsung is finally rolling out Wear OS 7 to older Galaxy Watch models with its One UI 9 Watch update.

Samsung’s One UI 9 Watch update is largely focused on new health features including Vitals, Heart Health Score, Daily Cardio Load, Fitness Index, and improvements to both Sleep Coaching and Sleep Apnea alerts.

“Vitals” is perhaps the most useful day-to-day addition, with Samsung Health collecting data on key vital signs such as heart rate, heart rate variability, respiratory rate, skin temperature, and blood oxygen and establishing a range so you can see when your vitals are out of the usual range.

One UI 9 Watch is based on Wear OS 7, and also includes an improved Compass app with “Track Back,” a feature that helps users to follow their route back to a recorded point.

There’s also a new “Portrait Watch Face” that can adjust the clock face around the subject of a photo.

Samsung is, as of today, officially rolling out One UI 9 Watch with Wear OS 7 to the Galaxy Watch 8 and Galaxy Watch 8 Classic. Meanwhile, the update is in beta for several other older models which will be updated soon including:

The Galaxy Watch 5 series and Watch FE should also be getting the update, but no beta is available for those devices. Samsung’s first Wear OS watches, the Galaxy Watch 4 series, will not get the update.

#10451. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."
